Provisioning for retail customers

Provisions are assessed in accordance with the Group's provisioning
policies The majority of retail forbearance takes piace in the performing
book and, for the purposes of the latent loss provisions, these constitute
@ separate risk pool They are subject to higher provisioning rates than
the remainder of the performing book These rates are reviewed regularly
In both divisions Once forbearance Is granted, the account contmues to
be assessed separately for latent provisioning for 24 months (UK Retail
only) or untd the forbearance penod expires After that point the account
18 no longer separately identifted for latent provistoning [n the non-
performing portfolio, assets are grouped into homogeneous portfolios
sharing similar credit characteristics according to the asset type Further
charactenstics such as LTVs, arrears status and default vintage are also
cansidered when assessing recoverable amount and calculating the
related provision requirement Whilst non-performing forbearance retail
loans do not form a separate risk pool, the LGD models used to calculate
the collective mpatrment provision will be affected by agreements made
under forbearance arrangements

Impairment loss provision methodology

A financial asset or portfolio of financial assets is impatred and an
impairment loss incurred if there is objective evidence that an event or
events since intial recognition of the asset has adversely affected the
amount or timing of future cash flows from the asset

For retail loans, which are segmented into collective, homogenous
portiohos, time-based measures, such as days past due, are typically
used as evidence of impairment For these porttoltos, the Group
recognises an impairment at 90 days past due

For corporate portfolios, given ther complexity and nature, the Group
tehes not only on time-based measures, but also on management
judgement to dentfy evidence of smpairment Other factors considered
may include significant financial difficulty of the borrower a breach of
contract a loan restructunng a probable bankruptcy and any observable
data indicating a measurable decrease in estimated future cash flows

57

Financial review Risk and balance sheet management conunued

Early problem identification and problem debt management continued
Depending on vanous factors as explained below, the Group uses one of
the following three different methods to assess the amount of provision
required tndividual, collective, and latent

¢ Individually assessed provisions - Provisions required for individually
significant impaired assets are assessed on a case-by-case basis If
there 1s objective evidence that an impairment loss has been
incurred the amount of the loss is measured as the difference
between the assets carrying amount and the present value of the
estimated future cash flows discounted at the financial asset's
onginal effective interest rate Future cash flows are estimated
through a case-by-case analysis of individually assessed assets

This assessment takes into account the benefit of any
guarantees or other collateral held The value and tming of cash
flow receipts are based on available estimates in conjunction
with facts available at that time Timings and amounts of cash
flows are reviewed on subsequent assessment dates, as new
information becomes available The asset continues to be
assessed on an individual basis until itis repard in full
transterred to the performing portfolio or wntten-off

* Collectively assessed provisions - Provisions on impaired credits
below an agreed threshold are assessed on a portfolio basis to
reflect the homogeneaus nature of the assets The Group segments
impaired credits in its collectively assessed porttohas according to
asset type, such as credit cards, personal loans, mortgages and
smaller homogenous wholesale portfolios, such as business or
commercial banking A further distinction is made between those
Impaired assets in collections and thase i recoveries (refer to
Problem debt management on page 55 tor a discussion of the
collections and recovenes functions)

The provision is determined based on a quantitative review of
the relevant portfolio, taking account of the level of arrears, the
value of any secuntty, histoncal and projected cash recovery
trends over the recovery penod The provision also incorporates
any adjustments thal may be deemed appropriate given current
economic and credit conditions Such adjustments may be
determined based on a review of the current cash collectians
profile performance agaunst histoncal trends, updates to metne
inputs including model recalibrations, and monitonng of
operational processes used In managing exposures, including
the time taken to process non-performing expasures

Latent loss provisions - A separate approach 's taken for provisions
heid agasnst impairments in the performing portfolio that have been
incurred as a result of events occurring before the balance sheet
date but which have not been tdentilied at the balance sheet date

“unaud ted

58

The Group’s methodologies to estimate latent loss provistons reflect

© — the probability that the performing customer will default - historical
loss experience, adjusted, where appropnate to take into account
current economic and credit conditians, and

© the emergence pertod, defined as the period between an impairment |
event occurnng and a loan being identified and reported as |
impaired 1

Emergence penods are estimated at a portfolio level and reflect the

portiahe product charactenstics such as the repayment terms and the

duration of the loss mitigation and recovery processes They are based

on internal systems and processes within the partcular portfolio and are

reviewed regularly !

Provisions and impa:ments

Key points

* Commercial real estate continues to be the primary sector driving
the Ulster Bank Group non-performing loan book A reduction over
the year of £1 8 billion primarily reflects Ulster Bank's continuing
Strategy ta reduce concentration nsk to this sector

* — The outlook for the property sector remains challenging While there
may be some signs of stabilisation in main urban centres, the
outlook continues to be negative for secondary property locations on
the island of Ireland

During the year, Ulster Bank experrenced further migration of
commercial real estate exposures to its problem management
framework where various measures may be agreed to assist
customers whose loans are performing but who are expenencing
temporary financial difficulties For further details on Wholesale
renegotiations refer to page 52

Asset quality

The asset quality analysis presented below is based on the Group s
internal asset quality ratings which have ranges tor the probability of
default, as set out below Customers are assigned credit grades, based
on various credit grading models that reflect the key drivers of default for

For portfolios secured on residential praperty these models typically
include measures of delinquency and loan to value as well as other
differentiating characteristics such as bureau score, product features or
associated account performance information

Asset-backed securities

introduction

NatWest Group's credit market activities gave nse to nsk concentrations
in asset-backed secunties (ABS) NatWest Group has exposures to ABS,
which are predominantly debt secunties, but can also be held in
denvative forrn ABS have an interest in an underlying pool of referenced
assets The naks and rewards of the referenced poo! are passed onto
investors by the issue of securities with varying senionty by a special
purpose ently

Debt secures include residential mortgage-backed secunties (RMBS),
commercial mortgage-backed secunties (CMBS), collateralised debt
obhgations (CDOs), collateralised loan obligations (Ci.Os) and other
ABS in many cases, the risk associated with these assets is hedged by
credit denvatives The counterparties to some af these hedge
transactions are monoline insurers

The following table summarises the rating levels of carrying values of
these secunties Government sponsored or similar RMBS comprises
securities that are guaranteed or effectively guaranteed by the US.
government, by way of Its support for US federal agencies and
government sponsored enterpnses

NATWEST 2012

Residential morigage-backed secuntes

RMBS are secuntes that represent an interest in 4 portfoho of residential
mortgages Repayments made on the underlying mortgages are used to
make payments to holders of the RMBS The nsk of the RMBS will vary
pnmanty depending on the quality and geographic region im whieh the
underlying morigage assets are located and the credit enhancement of
the securitisation structure Several tranches of notes are issued, each
secured against the same portiolo of mortgages, but providing differing
levels of semonty to match the risk appetite of investors The most junior
(or equity) notes will suffer early capital and interest losses experienced
by the referenced morgage collateral, with each more senior note
benefiting frorn the protection provided by the subordinated notes below
Additional credit enhancements may be provided to the holder of senior
RMBS notes, including provided by monoline insurers

NatWest Group RMBS classifications include sub-prime and non-
conforming Non-conforming RMBS include Alt-A RMBS Classrfication
as sub-prime or Alt-A Is based on Fair Isaac Corporation (FICO) scores,
level of documentation and loan-to-value (LTV) ratios of the underlying
mortgage loans RMBS are classified as sub-prime if the mortgage
portfolio compnses loans with FICO scares between 500 and 650 with full
or kmited documentation Mortgages in Alt-A RMBS portfolios have FICO
scores of 640 to 720, limited documentation and an original LTV of 70%
to 100%

REIL, provisions and AFS reserves

NATWEST 2012

Risk elements in lending (REIL) comprises impatred loans and accruing joans past due 90 days ar more as to principal or interest Impaired loans are all
loans (including renegotiated loans) for which an impairment provision has been established, for collectively assessed loans, impairment loss provisions
are not allocated to individual loans and the entire portfolio is included i impaired leans Accruing foans past due 90 days or more comprise loans past

due 90 days where no impairment loss ts expected and those awaiting individual assessment A latent provision ts established for the latter

Provisions and AFS reserves methodology

The Group's consumer portfolios, which consist of high volume small
value credits, have highly efficient largely automated processes for
identifying problem credits and very short timescales, typically three
months, before resolution or adaption of various recovery methods
Carporate portfolos consist of higher value, lower volume credits, which
tend to be structured to meet individual customer requirements

Provisions are assessed on a case by case basis by experienced
Specialists with mput from profassianal valuers and accountants The
Group operates a transparent provisions governance framework, setting
thresholds to tngger enhanced oversight and challenge

Analyses of provisions are set out on page 101 to 102

Available-for-sale financial assets are mitially recognised at fair value plus
dwectly related transaction costs and are subsequently measured at fair
value with changes in fair value reported in owners equity until disposal
at which stage the cumulative gain or loss 1s recognised in profit or loss
When there is objective evidence that an available-for-sale financial asset
1s impaired, any decline in its fair value below original cost ts removed
from equity and recognised in profit or loss

The Group reviews its portfolios of available-for-sale financial assets for
evidence of impairment, which includes default or delinquency in interest
or pnncipal payments, significant financial difficulty of the issuer or
obligor, and it becorning probable that the issuer will enter bankruptcy or
other financial rearganisation However the disappearance af an active
market because an entity's financial struments are no longer publicly
Traded is not evidence of Impairment Furthermore, a downgrade of an
enbly’s credit rating 1s not, of self, evidence of impamment, although it
may be evidence of impairment when considered with other available
Infarmation A decline in the fair value of a financial asset below its cost
or amortised cost is not necassanly evidence af impairment Determining
whether objective evidence of impairment exists requires the exercise of
management judgement The unrecognised losses an the Group s
avatable-for-sale debt securities are concentrated tn its portfoltos of
mortgage-backed secunties The losses reflect the widening of credit
spreads as a result of the reduced market fiquidity in these securities and
the current uncertain macroeconomic outlook in the US and Europe The
underlying securities remain unimpaired

REIL flow statement
REIL are stated without giving effect to any security held that could
reduce the eventual loss should it occur or to any provisions marked

Potential problem loans (PPL) are loans for which an impainment event has taken place bul no impamnment loss is expected This category ts used for
advances which are not past due 90 days or revolving credit facies where identification as 90 days overdue 1s not feasible

Market risk

Introduction

Market risk arises from fluctuations tn interest rates, foretgn currency,
credit spreads, equity prices, commodity prices and risk related factors
such as market volatilities The RBS Group manages market risk within
its trading and non-trading portfolios through a comprehensive market
risk management framework This control framework includes qualitative
and quantitative guidance in the form of comprehensive policy
statements, dealing authonties, limits based on but not limited to, value-
at-risk (VaR), stressed VaR (SVaR), stress testing and sensitivity
analyses

Governance

Business structure

NatWest Group s principally engaged in the purchase sale and financing
of US Treasury, US Agency, asset-backed, corporate debt, equity
secunties and the execution clearance of exchange traded futures and
options on fulures contracts The NatWest Group transacts pnmarily with
inshtutional counterparties and government sponsared entities through its
US trading subsidiary, RBS Securties Inc The majonty of NatWest
Group's market nsk exposure is in Markets

The NatWest Group undenakes transactions m financial instruments that
are traded or cleared on an exchange, including interest rate swaps,
futures and options Holders of exchange traded instrurnents provide
margin on a daily basis with cash or other secunty at the exchange

The NatWest Group also undertakes transactons in financial instruments
that are traded over-the-counter rather than on a recognised exchange
These instruments range from commoditised transachons in denvaiive
markets, to trades where the specific terms are tallored to meet customer
requirements

Organisation structure

Independent oversight and support is provided to the divisions by the
Global Head of Market & Insurance Risk, assisted by the RBS Group and
divisional market nsk teams The head of each divisian, assisted by a
divisional market isk management team, is accountable for all market
nsks associated with its activites The Global Market Risk Committee
reviews and makes recommendations concerning the market ask profile
across the RBS Group, including nsk appetite, ask policy, models,
methodglagy and markat nsk development issues The committee meets
quarterly and 1s chaired by the Global Head of Market & Insurance Risk
Attendees include respective divisional market nsk managers and RBS.
Group Market Risk

Regulatory nsk

Trading actvities wil indirectly be impacted by regulatory proposals that
will change market participants behaviours These are discussed in more
detail in the Regulatory risk section (refer to page 123) Developments
specific to market nsk include the Fundamental Review of the Trading
Book (FRTB) and the Fundamental Review of the Secuntisation
Treatments The FATB remains at a conceptual stage and there is
currently insufficient practical detail available to provide a meaningful
assessment of what may eventually be implemented The Base!
Committee's review of the treatment of securitisation positions is further
advanced and the RBS Group is currently reviewing how it can
participate to assess the impact on trading book activities

"unaudited

NATWEST 2012

Risk measurement

Key principles

The RBS Group's qualitative market risk appetite is set out in policy
statements, which outline the governance, responsibilities and
requirements surrounding the tdentification, measurement analysis,
management and communication of market risk ansing from the trading
and non-trading investment activities of the RBS Group All teams
involved in the management and control of market risk are required to
fully comply with the policy statements to ensure the RBS Group is not
exposed to market nsk beyond the qualitative and quantitative sk
appetite The control framework covers the following principles

* Clearly detined responsibilities and authonties tor the primary
groups involved in market nsk management in the RBS Group,

* — An independent market nsk management process,

* — Daily montonng, analysis and reporting of market sk exposures
against market risk hmits

* — Clearly defined hrut structure and escalation process in the event of
a market nsk himit excess

*  Amarket isk measurement methodology that captures correlation
effects and allows aggregation of market nsk across risk types,
markets and busmess lines,

* Use of VaR as a measure of the one-day and SVaR as a measure
of the ten-day market nsk exposure of all trading positions,

* Use of non-VaR based bmits and other controls,

* — Use of stress testing and scenano analysis to support the market
nak measurement and nek management process by assessing how
portiolos and global busmess lines perfonn under extreme market
conditions,

* Use of back-testing as a diagnostic tool to assess the accuracy of
the VaR model and other nsk management techniques,

* Adherence fo the nsks not in VaR framework to dently, quantity
and capitalise risks nat captured within the VaR model, and

* — A product approval process that requires market risk teams to
assess and quantity market rsk associated with proposed new
Praduots

Risk appetite’

The Executive Risk Forum (ERF) approves the quantilative market ask
appetite for trading and non-trading activites The Global Head of Market
& Insurance Risk, under delegated authority from the ERF sets and
populates a limt framework, which is cascaded down through legal entity,
division, business and desk lavel market nsk limits

VaFt - 1s a technique that produces estimates of the potential change in
the market value of a portfolio over a specified time hanzon at a given
conhdence level For intemal nsk management purposes, the NatWest
Group s VaR assumes a time honzon of one trading day and a
contidence level of 99% The NatWest Group's VaR model is based on a
histoncal simulation model, utilising data from the previous two years.

The VaR model has been approved by the FSA to calculate regulatory
capital for the trading book tor those legal entities under its jurisdicbon
Within NatWest Group, these legal entites are National Westminster
Bank Ple RBS Financral Products Inc and RBS Secunties Inc
Regulatory VaR differs from the internal VaR as it 1s based on a ten-day
holding penod The approval covers general market risk in interest rate,
foreign exchange, equity and specified commodity products and specific
risk in interest rate and equity products

The VaR model is an important market risk measurement and control
tool Itis used for determining a significant component of the market nsk
capital and, as such itts regularly assessed The main approach
employed to asses the ongoing model performance is back-testing, which

"unaudited
106

counts the number of days when a lass (as defined by the FSA) exceeds
the corresponding daly VaR estimate, measured at a 99% confidence
level The FSA categorises a VaR model as green, amber or red A green
model status Is consistent with a good working mode! and 1s achieved for
models that have four or fewer back-testing exceptions in a 12-month
period For the NatWest Group's trading book, a green model status was
maintained throughout 2012

The NatWest Group’s VaR should be interpreted in {ight of the limitations
of the methodology used, as follows

* — Historical simulation VaR may not provide the best estimate of future
market movements it can only provide a forecast of portfoho losses
based on events that occurred in the two-year ime senes
Therefore events that are more severe than those in the historical
data series are not represented

* The use of a 99% contidence level does not reflect the extent of
potential losses beyond that percentile

* The use of a one-day time honzon will not fully capture the profit and
loss imphcations of positions that cannot be liquidated or hedged
within one day

* The NatWest Group computes the VaR of trading portfolios at the
close of business Positions may change substantially dunng the
course of the trading day and, if so, intra-day profit and losses will
be incurred

These limitations mean that the NatWest Group cannot guarantee that
losses will not exceed the VaR.

During 2012, an improved methadology was implemented for mterest
rates, to more realistically represent the distribution of rate changes The
enhanced model intraduced a level-dependent scaling methodology for
interest rates, which removes the overestimation of rate fluctuations in
regimes of dechning rates and leads to a swifter adaptation to changing
circumstances im tres of increasing rates In NatWest Group, at the
point of rmplementation the impact on the trading VaR was an increase of
£0 2 milhon, while the interest rate VaR saw a decrease of £3 8 million
The non-trading total and interest rate VaR both decreased by

£0 6 milbon

SVaR -1s applied to the trading portfolio and utilises data from a specific
one year penod of stress As with VaR, the technique produces estimates
of the potential change i the market value of a portiolio over a specified
time honzon at given confidence tevel For the purposes of calculating
regulatory SVaR, a time honzon of ten trading days is assumed and a
confidence level of 99%

in December 2012, the FSA confirmed the European Banking Authonty

SVaR where the worst one year period of stress 1s determined on a daily

Asks not in VaR (RNIV) - The RNIV framework has been developed to
quantify those market nsks not adequately captured by VaR and SVaR
methodologies The RNIV approach Is used for market nsks that fall
within the scope of VaR, but which are insufficiently captured by the
model methodology, for example due to the jack of sufficient historical
data These nsks are therefore assessed outside the VaR model

The RBS Group adopts two approaches to the quantification of nsks not
in VaR (RNIVs)

* Some RNIVs are quantified using a (standalone) VaR approach For
these RNIVs two values are calculated (1) the VaR RNIV and (11)
the SVaR RNIV

Some RNIVs are quantified using a stress scenarto approach For
these RNIVs, an assessment of ten-day extreme, but plausible,
market moves is used in combination with position sensitivities to
give a stress-type lass number - the stressed RNIV value

For each legal entity covered by the FSA VaR madel waiver, all RNIVs
are aggregated to obtain the following three measures (1) Total VaR
RNIV, (1) Total SVaR RNIV, and (tt) Total stressed RNIV

In each case, no allowance is made for potential diversification in respect
of maternal RNIVs

ineremental nsk charge (IRC) - The IRC madel aims to quantify the
Impact of defaults and rating changes on the market value of bonds,
credit denvatves, and other related positions held in the trading book It
is calculated over a one year honzon to a 99 9% confidence level, and
therefore represents a 1-1n-1,000 toss over the following year The
modeling framework differentiates between the liquidity of different
underlying instruments, with a minimum fiquidity horizon of three months
I also captures basis nsks between differant praducts referencing the
same underlying credit (eg bonds and credit default swaps (CDS)), and
between simular products with different contractual terms (eg GOS in
different currencies) The portioho impact of correlated defaults and rating
changes 1s assessed with reference to the resufting market vatue change
of positions which is determined using stressed recovery rates and
modelled credit spread changes The average liquidity honzon at the year
end was 4 6 months

in 2012, the IRG model was enhanced further, 1) to better capture the nsk
characteristics of sovereign exposure migrations and defaults, and 1) to
align the recovery rates for sovereign exposures to the banking book
internal ratings based approach

All pnce risk (APR) - there was no requirement to calculate any APR

Market's correlation trading portfolio dunng 2012

Market risk Risk measurement continued

Stress testing’

The RBS Group undertakes daily stress testing to identify the potential
losses In excess of VaR Stress testing ts used to calculate a range of
trading book exposures which result trom severe and extreme market
events Stress testing measures the impact of exceptional changes in
market rates and prices on the fair value of the RBS Group s trading and
avarlable-for-sale portfolios The RBS Group calculates sensitivity
analysis, histortcal stress tests and bottom-up stress testing

Sensitivity analysis measures the sensitivity of the current portfolio of
positions to defined market nsk factor movemenis These stresses are of
a smaller magnitude compared to histoncal or bottom-up stress testing
and are subject to the RBS Group Market Risk hmut framework

Historical stress tests calculate the changes in the portfolio valuations
that woutd be generated if the extreme market movements that occurred
during significant historical market events were repeated Histoncal stress
tests also form part of the RBS Group Market Risk mut framework

Bottom-up stress testing requires analysis of the market nsk exposures
by nsk factors and different liquidity honzons, to identify the key risks
Stresses for these risks are then designed following consultation with nsk
managers, economists and iront ofice The tests may be based on an
economic scenario that is translated into nsk factor shocks by an
econommst or by risk managers and front office as a means of assessing
the vulnerabilities of ther book

The Global Market Risk Stress Testing Committee reviews and discusses
all matters relating to market nsk stress testing Stress test exposures are
discussed with senor management and relevant information ts reported
to the RBS Group Risk Committees the ERF and the Board Breaches in
the R&S Group’s market nsk stress testing kmuts are monitored and
reported

Reverse stress testing is designed to assess the plausibility of scenanos
derived by stressing market nsk factors unti the loss reaches a given
threshold Market Risk contributes to the firm wide, cross risk reverse
stress tests

In addition to VaR and stress testing, the RBS Group calculates a wide
range of sensitivity and position nsk measures, for example interest rate
ladders or option revaluation matrices These measures provide valuable
additional controls, often at individual desk or strategy level

Pneing models”

Pricing models are developed and owned by the front office Where
pricing models are used as the basis of books and records valuations,
they are subyect to oversight and approval by asset level modelled
product review committees These committees pnoritse models for
independent validation by GRA taking into consideration both the
matenality of nsk booked against the model and an assessment of the
degree of mode! nsk (ie valuation uncertainty anging from choice of
modelling assumptions) GRA review aims to quantify model nsk by
comparing mode! outputs against those of altemative independently
developed models the results of which are used by Market Risk to inform
nsk limits and by Finance to inform model reserves

Marking-to-market

To ensure that the nsks associated with trading activity are reflected in
the financial and management statements, assets and abilities in the
trading book are measured at their fair value Any profits or losses on the
revaluation of positions are recognised in the income statement on a daily
basis

The fai value 1s the amount at which the instrument could be exchanged,
1n a Current transaction between willing parties The fai values are
determined following (AS 39 ‘Financial Instruments Recognition and
Measurement’ guidance, which requires banks to use quoted market

prices or valuation techniques (models) that make the maximum use of '
observable inputs

When marking-to-market using a model, the valuation methodologies
must be approved by all stakeholders (trading, finance, market risk '
model development and model review) pror to use tor profit and loss and

nsk management purposes

Traders are responsible for marking-to-markel them trading book
positions on a daily basis Traders can either

* — directly mark a position with a price (e g spot foreign exchange), or

* indirectly mark a position through the marking of inputs to an
approved model, which will in tum generate a price

Country risk

Introduction*

Cauntry risk 1s the nsk of matenal losses ansing trom significant country-
specilic events such as sovereign events (default or restructunng)
economic events (contagion of sovereign default to other parts of the
economy, cycheal economic shock), political events (transfer or
convertibility restnctons, expropnation or nationalisation), and confhet
Such events have the potential to affect elements of NatWest Group's
credit portioho that are directly or indirectly Inked to the country in
question and can also give nse to market, hquidity, operational and
franchise nsk-related losses

External environment"

Country nsk, notably in the eurozone, remained elevated in 2012
particularly in the first half of the year Ecanomic growth projections were
lowered, predominantly for Europe, but also for a number of major
emerging markets However, important first steps towards acheving
longer-term stabilisation in the eurozone led to some notable easing of
cnsis risks Growth data from major non-European economies, such as
China, were more encouraging towards the end of the year The ability of
Policymakers to tackle fiscal challenges and restore confidence and
growth in both the US and Europe will be a key factor in determining the
pace of recovery

Eurozone risks

Eurozone risks continued to dominate, as concerns about the rmpact of
banking sector problems on government balance sheets led to further
capital flight from periphery countries and a nse in sovereign bond yields
unt August, particularly for Spain To break the feedback loop between
banks and their sovereigns, eurozone leaders agreed at their June
summit that the European Stability Mechanism (ESM), the eurozone’s
permanent crisis fund, could lend to banks directly once a single
eurozone-wde banking regulator had been established They also
approved the provision by the ESM of significant financial support to
Spain to recapitaiise its banks

In the second half of the year, the ESM became fully operational and the
European Centra! Bank (ECB) announced a major new facility, Outnght
Monetary Transactions This facility allows secondary market purchases
by the ECB of bonds issued by eurozone sovereigns that are subject to a
European Union (EU)/nternational Monetary Fund (IMF) support
programme Following these steps sovereign bond yields fell markedly

Meanwhile, tn Greece, private sector claims on the government were
restructured in early 2072, but political nsks remained acute as two
successive parhamentary elections eventually resulted in a narrow victory
tor the pro-ballout New Democracy party As the electoral process
delayed polcy implementation and the recession contrary to earier
expectations, deepened further, additional reforms became necessary
and the European Commussion, the IMF and the EGB (known collectively
as the Troika) further eased Greece s targets

NATWEST 2012

Elsewhere, Ireland continued to make progress towards targets set out in
its Troika programme, notably allowing the government to resume a
degree of market financing Taiks with the European authorities on ways
to relieve the government of some of the costs of past banking sector
support continued, resulting in a favourable restructuring of the Anglo
insh promissory note in early 2013, reducing related fiscal costs
somewhat Notwithstanding these developments, Insh growth remained
very weak and rellant on external demand Portugal also made progress
in & Number of areas, though had greater structural constraints to

year, Cyprus also entered negotiations with the EU and IMF on a support
programme The eurozone as a whole entered recession im the second
half of the year, although divergence within the currency union continued,
with the core considerably stronger than the penphery

Emerging markets

Emerging markets performed better on the whale In developing Asia, the
economies of China and India both continued to slow from a strong base,
but nsks remained held in check by healthy external balance sheets

Emerging countries in Europe started to be affected by very weak growth
In the eurozone, with the most export-focused economies being worst hit
However, countnes that took significant action in the wake of the financial
crisis to stabilise their banking sectors, saw an easing of risk Turkey was
upgraded by one rating agency to investment grade

General political instability seen in the Middle East and North Afnca in
2011 moderated in 2012 in most countnes except Syna, although

Bahrain, Gulf Cooperation Council countnes were generally more stable,

Latin America continued to be charactensed by greater stability, due to

Outlook

Overall, the outlook for 2013 remains challenging with risks iikely to
remain elevated but divergent Much will depend on the success of EU
efforts to contain contagion from the sovereign crisis (where downside
nsks are high) and on whether growth headwinds in larger advanced
economies, particularly the US and Japan, persist Emerging market
balance sheet risks remain lower, despiie structural and politcal

Limits on total exposure are set for individual counties based on a risk

situation and quilook, as well as the RBS Group's portiolio composition in
that country Sub-lmits are set on medium-term (greater than one year)
exposure since this exposure can, by nature, not be reduced as rapidly
as short-term exposure in the event of detenoration of a country’s
creditworthiness

Dunng 2012 1m addition to all emerging markets and the vuinerable

Redenomination nsk

The overail impact of redenomiation nsk on the NatWest Group 1s
difficult to determine with certamty, but would be shaped by the scope
and reach of any new legislation introduced by an exiting country, its
applicability to the facility documentation, and whether there are any
appropnate offsets to the exposures For the purposes of estimating
funding mismatches ai risk of redenomination (detailed below), NatWest
Group takes, as tts starting pomt balance sheet exposure as defined on
page 113 and excludes exposures at low nsk of redenomination The
latter are identified through consideration of the relevant documentation,
particulary the currency of exposure, governing law court of jurisdiction,
precise definition of the contract currency (for euro facilites), and location
of payment NatWest Group aiso deducts offsets for provisions taken and
liabilities that would be expected to redenommnate at the same time

A redenomination event would also be accompanied by increased credit
nsk, for two reasons First, capital contrals would likely be Introduced in
the affected country, resuliing in any non-redenominated assets,
Including non euro assets, potentially becommg harder to service
Second, a sharp devaluation could imply payment difficulhes for
counterparties with large debts denominated in foreign currency and
counterparties that are heawly dependent on imports

Definitions

Lending - Comprises gross loans and advances to central and local
government (Govt) central banks, including cash balances, other banks
and financial institutions (FI), incorporating overdraft and other short-term
facilites, corporates, in large part loans and leases, and individuals,
compnsing mortgages, personal loans and credit card balances Lending
includes nsk elements in fending

Fisk elements in lending (REIL) - Comprises impaired loans and accruing
past due 90 days or more as to principal or interest Impaired loans are
all loans (including renegotiated) for which an impairment provision has
been established Accruing past due 90 days or more compmse loans
past due 90 days where no impairment loss is expected and those
awaiting individual assessment A fatent provision is established for the
latter

Debt securities - Compnse secuntes classified as available-tor-sale
({AFS), loans and receivables (LAR), held-tor-trading (HFT) and
designated as at far value through profit or loss (DFV) All debt secunties
other than LAR securities are carried at fair value LAR debt securities
are carned at amortised cost less impairment HFT debt secunties are
presented as grass long positions (including DFV secuntes) and short
positions per country Impaiment losses and exchange differences
relating to AFS debt secunties together with interest, are recognised in
the income statement Other changes in the fair value of AFS securities
are reported within AFS reserves, which are presented gross of tax

NATWEST 2012

Dervatives (net) Comprise the mark-to-market (mtm) value of such
contracts after the effect of legally enforceable netting agreements but
before the effect of collateral Figures shown include the effect of
counterparty netting used within the regulatory capital model

Repos (net) - Comprises the mtm value of repo and reverse repo
contracts after the effect of legally enforceable netting agreements and

collateral Counterparty netting is applied within the regulatory capital
model used

in addition and as a memorandum item, the mtm value of denvatives and
repas gross of netting referred to above are disclosed

Balance sheet Compnises lending. debt securties, denvatives (net) and
repo (net) exposures, as defined above

Off-balance sheet - Compnises letters of credit, guarantees other
contingent obligations and committed undrawn facilities

Government - Compnses central, regional and local government

Asset quality (AQ) - For the probabihty of default range relating to each
internal asset quality band, refer to page 78

Eurozone penphery - Comprises Ireland, Spam, Italy Portugal, Greece
and Cyprus

Other eurozone - Compnses Austra, Estona Finland, Malta, Slovakia
and Slovenia

Other risks

Operational risk*

Operational risk 1s the risk of loss resulting from inadequate or fated
internal processes people and systems, or from external events [tis an
integral and unavoidable part of the Group’s business as it's inherent in
the processes It operates to provide services to customers and meet
strategic objectives

Operational risk management

The objective of operational nsk management is not to remove
operational risk altogether, but to manage tt to an acceptable level, taking
Into account the cost of minimising the msk against the resultant reduction
In exposure Strategies to manage operational risk include avoidance,
transfer, acceptance and mitigation by controls

in 2012, the RBS Group continued to make good progress in enhancing
Its operational risk framework and risk management capabilities Details
of developments undertaken and planned are set out below along with
the key processes through which the RBS Group manages operational
risk

in 2013, through further embedding the enhanced operational risk
framework and tools and improving framewark linkages, operational risk
will be managed on a more forward-looking basis

Governance, structure and risk appetite

Governance and structure

RBS Group Operational Risk is an independent function reporting to the
Head of Restructunng & Risk It's responsible for the design and
maintenance of the operational nsk policy standards

The standards which are incorporated in the RBS Group Policy
Framework (GPF), provide the direction for delivering effective
operational risk management and are designed to allow the consistent
identification, assessment management, monitoring and reporting of
operational risk across the RBS Group

The Operational Risk Executive Committee acts upon all operational isk
matters, and reviews and monitors the operational nsk profile across the
Group, tn line with risk appetite [tf oversees, manages and monitors
operational risk strategies and frameworks, and reviews operational risk
policy It escalates and reports necessary items to the RBS Group Risk
Committee

Operational risk appetite, policy and frameworks are reviewed regularly at
the Executive Risk Forum to satisfy oversight responsibilities and, as
appropriate, other senior committees

Risk appetite

The RBS Group's operational risk appetite statement is agreed by the
RBS Group Board It compnses a number of specific measures of nsk,
such as

e The maximum operational risk losses the RBS Group is prepared to
accept For 2012, this was expressed as a percentage of the RBS
Group’s estimated gross income but will transtton to a more
forward-looking expected loss measure during 2013 following the
development of the operational ask model, and

e Aggregate loss targets at specific confidence levels

* unaudited

NATWEST 2012

To confirm thai the RBS Group operates within the set risk appetite, the
high-level statement is supplemented by specific tolerances for different
types of operational risk The GPF sets out how to manage risk within
acceptable limits, which in tum enables the RBS Group to operate within
the overall risk appetite and the specific tolerances

Operational risk cycle and key management toots
The operational nsk cycle compnses four stages

« = identification of nsks,
« assessment or measurement of the scale of risks,

# management or control of risks to prevent ther recurrence or
minimise the potental impact and

« = moniterng and reporting of risks

Although the operational nsk tools encompass all stages of the nsk cycle,
they can be broadly categorised as follows

Identification and assessment

Risk and control assessments

Risk and control assessments are used to identify and assess matenal
operational risks and key controls across all business areas To provide a
consistent categorisation of nsks and controls across the RBS Group and
to support identification of risk cancentrations, all risks and controls are
mapped to the RBS Group-wide risk taxonomy and the newly developed |
control catalogue |

The process is designed to contimm that risks are effectively managed in
line with stated nsk appetite, prioritised and documented Controls are

tested frequently to verify and validate that they remain fit for purpose

and operate effectively

Risk assessments are often conducted in a workshop environment,

bringing together subject matter experts and key stakeholders from

across the division and key functons This approach has !ed to a more

complete view of the nsk profile and more informed decisions '

During 2013 the focus will be on the continued implementation and
embedding of risk assessments across the RBS Group This includes the
strengthening of inks between risk assessments and other elements of
the RBS Group operational nsk framework In addition, risk assessments
will increasingly be utilised to identify single points of failure

New product risk assessment process

The RBS Group's new product risk assessment process is designed to
identify, assess and approve the nsks associated with new products pnor
to launch

Several process enhancements were made during 2012 which
strengthened the interaction between business, nsk and specialist areas
Reporting has been established and wark has started to enhance the
inventory of products provided by the RBS Group

Other nsks Operational risk* continued

Scenario analysis

Scenano analysis ts used to assess the possible impact of extreme but
plausible operational nsk loss events It provides a forward-looking basis
for managing exposures beyond the RBS Group's nsk appetite The
methodology provides a structured and consistent approach to scenario
scoping and measurement During 2012, the portigho of scenarios was
jurther enhanced to increase coverage of the matenal asks to which the
RBS Group 1s exposed

Scenano analysis is an important component in the operational nsk
framework, providing senior management with valuable insight into
systeme risk that could significantly impact the RBS Group's financial
performance ar reputation tf these events were to occur Using its
forward-looking nature, senior management crass-examines vanous risk
topics against a range of circumstances and assumptions, including
consideration of single points of failure

Scenarios are run in a workshop environment, bringing business, nsk and
control experts together thereby ensunng that nsk management ts
approached holstically They include RBS Group-wide themes, which are
ied by RBS Group Operational Risk and alow the RBS Group to assess
the impacts of pan-divisional nsks and macroeconomic stresses (e g
eurozane distress)

Stress testing

The RBS Group further refined its approach to assessing the impact of
the economic cycle on its operational risk losses by specifically assessing
the impact of the FSA's published Anchor li scenario, which describes a
series of country-specific shocks around the world on

e = expected levels of operational nsk losses and
® capital adequacy requirements for operational risk

The impact of the FSA Anchor Il scenano on the RBS Group's
operational risk capital as calculated under the standardised approach,
was also projected based on the outputs of the RBS Group's stress-
testing exercises

Operational risk impacts are also assessed based on additional economic
stress scenarios developed internally

Capital model development

The RES Group has continued with the development of its statistical
modelling capability for operational nsk based on the requirements set
out under the Basel il advanced measurement approach The model
considers intemal and external loss data as well as scenarios and
business environment and internal control factors The pnmary use of the
moadel will be to contnbute to the economic capital calculation and to help
test the RBS Group's capital adequacy requirement Embedding ot the
model outputs 1s continuing in 2013

Management, monitoring and reporting

issues management

The objective of the operational nsk issues management tramework 1s
the adoption of a consistent approach to the identification capture,
classification, monitoring, closure and acceptance of operational nsk
issues and associated actions across the RBS Group

This element of the operational nsk framework continues to be enhanced
in areas such as analysis of common issues on an aggregated basis
across the RBS Group to identity emerging trends and improvements to
the quality of data captured

Event and loss data management

Event and loss data management covers the discavery, escalation,
capture, investigation, approval and closure, and reporting and analysis
of operational risk events and loss data It also provides for clear, simple,
quick and consistent communication of operational nsk events that meet
defined threshold critena to those members of the RBS Group's senior
management and execulives who need to know of these events

The RBS Graup has continued to focus on the timely, accurate capture of
operational nsk losses, the use of a single RBS Group-wde repository,
and the escalation of material operational nsk events This has resulted in
enhanced completeness and accuracy of the RBS Group's internal loss
data, and transparency of operational risk events, which allows the RBS
Group to manage its operational risk proftie more effectively

The event and loss data process will continue to evalve to keep apace
with changing regulatory and industry standards regarding the collection
of intemal loss data

insurance

The RBS Group purchases insurance to provide the business with
financial protection against specific losses and to comply with statutory or
contractual requirements Insurance is used to help manage the RBS
Group s exposures, providing protection against financial loss once a nsk
has crystallised

Momtoring and reporting

Monitonng and reporting forms an integral part of all of the RBS Groups
operational nsk management processes, which are designed to ensure
that nsks and issues are identified, escalated and managed on a timely
basis Exposures for each division are reparted through monthly nsk and
control reporis, which provide detail on the nsk exposures and action
plans

Enhancements made during 2012 include single-source extraction and
publication of operational nsk data across the RBS Group, such as
operational nsk events and losses This has resulted in consistent and
higher quality mfarmation far the purposes of oversight, challenge and
operational risk management

* = complance with the Group Policy Framework and key

« «compliance with the requirements of the UK Corporate Governance

Regulatory risk*

Regulatory nsk Is the risk of matenal loss or hability, legal or regulatory
sanctions, or reputationa! damage, ansing from the fatture to comply with
(or adequately plan for changes to) relevant official sector policy, jaws,
regulations, or major industry standards, tn any location In which the
Group operates The RBS Group believes that maintaining a strong
tegulatory nsk framework is fundamental to protecting sustainable growth,
rebuilding Its reputation and maintaining stakeholder confidence

The regulatory environment remained highly challenging during 2012, as
policymakers and regulators continued to strengthen regulation and
supervision in response to the events of 2007/2008 and subsequent
economic and financial stress

The regulatory agenda largely tramed by the G20 but with many
instances of European Union (EU) and national initiatives, constitutes the
most sweeping set of changes seen in many decades At 31 December
2012, the RBS Group was managing some 105 major regulatory or
legislative policy initiatives During the year as a whole, tt had also
reviewed over 320 consultations in its core markets In addition to these
changes, many supervisory authonties also continued to intensify their
ongoing level of scrutiny and intervention

*unaudiied

These trends have posed multiple challenges for banking groups,
including the RBS Group namely

« tracking, analysing and engaging with policymakers on proposed
changes,

e = tmplementing change programmes to ensure comphance with new
requirements,

e revisiting strategy, business and operating models in response to
the new environment, and

« = driving through cultural and other changes to promote good |
business practice and to minimise enforcement risks |
|

Global requlatory developments

The global agenda continues to be guided by the G20, drawing on the
onginal action plan for strengthening financial stability agreed by G20
leaders at the November 2008 Washington summit Although policy
inttation at the G20 level is drawing to an end, a substantia! pipeline of
policy development remains in train and the Group does not anticipate
any easing of this for some time Dunng 2012, G20 countnes continued
to implement various elements of this action plan, and further endorsed it
at the G20 leaders summit held in Los Cabos, Mexico in June 2012 and
the finance minsters and central bank governors’ meetings, most
recently in Mexico City in Novernber 2012

Basel II!

Following pubhicatron by the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision in
December 2010 of rules for the new Basef III capital and Itquidity
framework, work during 2012 focused on finalising the remaining
elements of policy and prepanng for implementation Highhghts were

e Publication of results of the Basel II} monitoring exercise at 30 June
2011 (published April 2012) and at 31 December 2011 (published

EU regulatory developments

The EV regulatory agenda in 2012 continued to focus mainly on
prudential and market structure measures Retail issues also came under
increased focus Key highlights were as follows

The Lnkanen Review

In November 2011 the EU Commissioner for Internal Market and
Services, Michel Bamier, announced the establishment of a High-Level
Expert Group to cansider structural retorm of EU banks and in early 2012
it was convened under the chairmanship of Erkki Lukanen, the Governor
of the Bank of Finland The group was mandated to consider measures to
improve EU banks’ stabilty and efficiency In addition to any new
measures, it was tasked to look at ongoing structural reforms, including
the UK Independent Commission on Banking and the US ‘Volcker Rule

The Expert Group's proposals in October 2012 contained five
recommendations a ning-fence of trading book activities where they form
a significant part of a bank’s actiuty effective recovery and resolution
plans (with authorities empowered to require further structural reform If
that improves resolvability), specttic ‘bail-in instruments (rather than a
general bail-in power applied to existing flabilities), stncter capital
treatment of trading book and real estate exposures, and a number of
corporate governance risk management and remuneration proposals

The Commission ts considering the Expert Group’s recommendations
and has said that it will formaily respond by September 2013 Member
state views on the Expert Group's proposals, where expressed, have
been mixed The UK is meanwhile pushing ahead with implementation of
Its own ring-fencing reforms, as set out by the Independent Commisston
on Banking These go further than the Expert Group's proposals France
and Germany have also published draft legislation of their own on ring-
fencing, which focus mainly on separating out proprietary trading (but
allowing market making activities to remain within the deposit-taking
bank)

Crisis management and banking union proposals

In June 2012, the EU Commission published proposals for an EU-wide
recovery and resolution regime, providing for banks and authorities to
maintain plans for each firm, setting out measures to set nght or resolve
businesses should they face difficulties Authorities would receive a
number of powers to intervene in banks for these purposes, including
early intervention powers ahead of problems coming to light, and a
minimum set of tools to restructure or wind up a failed firm

Among the new tools is the power to ‘bail in’ senior creditors when
resolving a firm, to ensure losses are spread among shareholders and
creditors, without recourse to tax-payer funding Barled-in creditors take a
loss and become shareholders tn the new entity created

These proposais are likely to be agreed in 2013, with member states and
banks in compliance from 2015, and bail-in provisions fram 2018
Notwithstanding these developments, the euro-area cnsis continued to
develop and in July 2012 the President of the European Council,
Herman Van Rompuy, set out a road-map for further euro-area financial
Integration This aims to both resolve the current crisis and tackle
longstanding structural problems in the single-currency zone
Fundamental to these proposals are banking and fiscal umon and further
economic integration The President's banking union proposal comprises
a Single Supervisory Mechanism, and mutualisation of bank losses
through common deposit guarantee and resolution funding arrangements
The latter two elements are planned to follow agreement of the recovery
and resolution regime in 2013

in September, the Commission published Its proposal for a Single
Supervisory Mechanism, designating the European Central Bank (ECB)
as pnmary prudential supervisor for all euro-area banks, with opt-ins
available for EU member states outside the euro-area The Council of the
EU agreed to these proposals, with the proviso that the ECB would
directly supervise only larger banks and those in receipt of state ad,
while retaining some oversight of smaller banks that fall under the remit
of national supervisors

The European Parlament is now considering the proposals, with
agreement expected in early 2013 The ECB will not acquire full
supervisory authority until March 2014 and there is scope to delay this
Operational elements, such as how the ECB will be staffed, how it will
Interact with national supervisors and how tt will mplement its new
macro-prudential responsibilittes, remain to be seen More detail should
emerge during 2013

environment in the UK, with the establishment of the Financial Services
Canduct Business Unit (forerunner to the Financial Conduct Authority),

Reputational risk”

Reputational nsk ts the nsk of brand damage and/or financial loss due to
a failure to meet stakeholders’ expectations of the RBS Group's conduct
and performance

Stakeholders include customers, investors, rating agencies, employees,
suppliers, government, politicians, regulators, special interest groups,
consumer groups, media and the general public Brand damage can be
detnmental to the business in a number of ways, including an inability to
build or sustain business relationships with customers, low staff morale,
regulatory censure or reduced access to funding sources

One of the most fundamental stakeholder expectations is that a bank is
financially prudent, safe and sound The RBS Group has made significant
progress in meeting this expectation through the execution of its Strategic
Plan tn restructuring its balance sheet and improving its capital and
funding position Major reforms have also been made ta strengthen tts
rsk identification, evaluation and management processes Further work
remains but the RBS Group |s now In a much stronger financial position
to face challenges and uncertainties in its economic and operating
environment

Restoring the reputation of the RBS Group and the wider banking sector
1s butlt upon the role of banks as good companies that perform well for
their owners, regulators, employees and communites and, above all else,
serve their customers well

The RBS Group has put the focus on serving customers well at the heart
of its strategic objectives that, combined with a safe and sound bank, will
build a culture and reputation in line with our stakeholder expectations

There are stil legacy issues to work through, but dealing with them in an
open and direct manner Is a necessary part of the ability to move forward

The RBS Group s reputational nsk management framework Is aligned
with its strategic objectives and its risk appetite goal of maintaining
stakeholder confidence It is designed ta embed, at different points of
decision-making processes, a series of reputational filters and controls
that examine products, services and activities through the lenses of
sustainability transparency and fairness

This approach recognises that reputational nsk can arise across a range

of actions taken (or not taken) by the Group, as well as tts wider conduct,
polcies and practices Therefore, it is aligned with the management of a

range of nsk types that have a high reputational sensitivity

The RBS Group Board risk report contains a ‘top slice’ view of key
embedded risks, including a reputational risk impact assessment of each
key nsk In addition, the divistons report to their own committees and

reputational events according to the source

Business nsk*

Business risk is the risk that the Group suffers losses as a result of
adverse vanance in its revenues and/or costs relative to its business plan
and strategy Such vanance may he caused by mternal factors such as
volatility in pneing, sales volumes and input costs and/or by external

Business risk is tmpacted by other nsks the Group faces that may
contribute to the adverse changes in the Group's revenues and/or costs,
were these risks to crystallise Examples of such nsks include funding
nsk (through volatility in cast of funding), interest rate risk in the banking
book, operational nsk, conduct risk and reputational risk

The Group seeks to munimise tts exposure to business nsk subject to tts
wider strategic objectives Business risk ts identified, measured and
managed through the Group's planning cycles and performance
management processes Expected profiles for revenues and costs are
determined, on a bottom-up basis, through plans reflecting expectations
of the external environment and the Group's strategic pnonties These
profiles are tested against a range of stress scenarios and factors to
identify the key sk divers behind any potentral volatility, along with
Management actions to address and manage them

The Group operates a rolling forecast process which identifies projected
changes In, or nsks to operating profit and ensures appropriate actions
are taken

a key component in determining whether the RBS Group and Its

values over a 12-month honzon under various stresses and scenarios

Main scheme

The mast recent funding valuation, at 31 March 2010, was agreed during
2011 {t showed that the value of labilhes exceeded the value of assets
by £3 5 billion at 21 March 2010, a ratio of assets to habilities of 84% In
order to eliminate this deficit the ABS Group agreed to pay additional
contributions each year over the penod 2011 to 2018 These
contnbutions started at £375 millon per annum in 2011, increasing to
£400 million per annum in 2013 and from 2016 onwards will be further
increased tn line with price inflation Further details are provided in Note 4
of the consolidated accounts The next funding valuation is due at 31
March 2013

The assets of the Main scheme, which represent 85% of Group penston
plan assets at 31 December 2012, are invested in a diversified portfolio of
quoted and private equity government and corporate fixed interest and
index-linked bonds, and other assets including property and hedge funds
The trustee has taken measures to partially mitigate inflation and interest
tate nsks both by investing in suitable physical assets and by entering
into inflation and interest rate swaps The Main scheme also uses
derivatives within its portfollo to manage the allocation to asset classes
and to manage nsk within asset classes

tarry voting nights at general meetings of ordinary shareholders but are

Assat Protection Scheme

On 22 December 2009, the Group's parent company, The Royal Bank of
Scotland plc entered into an agreement, the Asset Protection Scheme
(APS), with HM Treasury acting on behalf of the UK Government, under
which it purchased credit protection over a portfolio of specified assets
and exposures (covered assets) from HM Treasury The portiolo of

192

covered assets included assets recorded on the Group's balance sheet

However, the Group was not enttled to benefit under thts contract and '
there were no arrangements to transfer the protection to the Group The

APS had therefore no effect on the Group's results and financial position

On 18 October 2012, the RBS Group exited the APS

The Board 1s the main decision-making forum for the Bank It has averall

high level strategic Issues and considering risk appetite, risk palictes and
risk management strategies in advance of these being considered by the

place in relation to business commitments across the Group

The roles of Chairman and Group Chiet Executive are distinct and
separate, with a clear division of responsibilities The Chairman leads the
Board and ensures the effective engagement and contribution of all
executive and non-executive directors The Group Chief Executive has
responsibihty for all Group businesses and acts in accordance with
authonty delegated by the Board The non-executive directars combine
broad business and commercial experience with independent and
objective judgement

The Group Audit Committee compnses at least three independent non-
executive directors and assists the Board in discharging Its
responsibilities for the disclosure of the financial affairs of the Group It
reviews the accounting policies, financial reporting and regulatory
comphance practices of the Group, the Group s system and standards of
internal controls, and monitors the Group's processes tor internat audit
and extemal audit and reviews the practices of the Divisional Risk and
Audit Committees

The Board Risk Committee compnses at least three independent non-
executive directors It provides oversight and advice to the Board on
current and potential future risk exposures of the Group and risk strategy
it reviews the Group's perlormance on risk appetite and oversees the
operation of the Group Policy Framework

The Group Performance and Remuneration Committee (formerly Group

18 responsible for overseeing and challenging how management is

Independent auditor’s report to the members of National Westminster Bank Plc

We have audited the financial statements of National Westminster Bank
Pic (the “Bank") and its subsidiaries (together the “Group’) for the year
ended 31 December 2012 which comprise the accounting policies, the
balance sheets as at 31 December 2012, the consolidated income
statement, the consolidated statement of comprehensive income, the
statements of changes in equity and the cash flaw statements for the
year ended 31 December 2012, the related Notes 1 to 40 and the
Information identified as ‘audited’, in the Risk and balance sheet
management section of the Financial review The financial reparting
framework that has been applied in thei preparation ts applicable law
and International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) as adopted by
the European Union and, as regards the Bank financial statements, as
applied in accordance with the provisians of the Companies Act 2006

This report is made solely to the Bank’s members, as a body, in
accordance with Chapter 3 of Part 16 of the Companies Act 2006 Our
audit work has been undertaken so that we might state ta the Bank's
members those matters we are required to state to them in an auditor s
report and for no other purpose To the fullest extent permitted by law, we
do not accept or assume responsibility to anyone other than the Bank
and the Bank’s members as a body, for our audit work, for this report, or
for the opinions we have formed

Respective responsibilities of directors and auditor

As explained more fully in the statement of directors’ responsibilites, the
directors are responsible for the preparation of thé financial statements
and for being satisfied that they give a true and fair view

Our responsibility is to audit and express an opinion on the financial
statements In accordance with applicable law and International Standards
on Auditing (UK and Ireland) Those standards require us to comply with
the Auditing Practices Board’s Ethical Standards for Auditors

Scope of the audit on the financial statements

An audit involves obtaining evidence about the amounts and disclosures
In the financial statements sufficient to give reasonable assurance that
the financial statements are free from material misstatement, whether
caused by fraud or error This includes an assessment of the
reasonableness of significant accounting estimates made by the directors
in the preparation of the financial statements, whether the accounting
policies are appropriate to the circumstances of the Bank and the Group
and have been consistently applied and adequately disclosed, and the
overall presentation of the financial statements In addition, we read all
the financial and non-financial information in the annual report to identify
maternal inconsistencies with the audited financial statements If we
become aware of any apparent material misstatements or inconsistencies
we consider the implications for our report

Opinion
In our opinion

e the financial statements give a true and fair view of the state of the
Group's and of the Bank's affairs as at 31 December 2012 and of
the Group's loss for the year then ended,

e — the Groups financial statements have been properly prepared in
accordance with IFRS as adopted by the European Union,

e the Bank's financial statements have been properly prepared in
accordance with IFRS as adopted by the European Union and as
appked in accordance with the provisions of the Companies Act
2006, and

e the fmancial statements have been prepared in accordance with the
requirements af the Companies Act 2006 and, as regards the Group
financial statements, Article 4 of the JAS Regulation

Separate opinion in relation to IFRS as issued by the 1ASB

As explained in the accounting policies, the Group in addition to
complying with its legal obligation to comply with IFRS as adopted by the
European Union, has also complied with the IFRS as issued by the
Intemational Accounting Standards Board (IASB)

In our opinion the Group financial statements comply with IFRS as issued
by the IASB

Opinion on other matters prescribed by the Compantes Act 2006
in our opmion the information given in the Report of the directors for the
